President Donald Trump’s approval rating has hit new lows as we approach the US midterms in November

The US midterm elections are on 3 November, with all 435 House of Representatives 
seats and 35 of the 100 Senate seats up for election. Worries about the cost of living and 
the impact of AI on job security are regularly cited as key factors driving sentiment, 
while support for the conflict with Iran has wavered. Opinion polls currently suggest the 
Democrats lead by around eight percentage points nationally, and President Donald 
Trump’s own approval rating has hit new lows, leading to Republican Party concerns 
about a demotivated electoral base. 
Pollsters and prediction markets anticipate a Democrat takeover of Congress, but 
surprises do happen in US politics. While the midterms rarely lead to radical changes in 
policy direction, the result could significantly constrain President Trump, putting more 
focus on foreign and trade policy. We look at what the possible outcomes could mean. 
Midterm election scenarios

Source: ING

US midterm elections: 3 scenarios for 
markets  
Intensifying cost-of-living concerns, linked to the price of motor fuel and the war with 
Iran, have boosted the Democrats' chances of regaining control of Congress, but nothing 
is guaranteed in US elections. We look at the three possible scenarios and what they 
may mean for the economy and markets

### Page 13

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

13 
Scenario 1: A Republican revival 
Republicans retain control of both the House and the Senate 
This is seemingly the least likely outcome at the current juncture, given the state of the 
opinion polls. Nonetheless, if it happens, President Trump will see it as justification for his 
America First strategy. With re-election no longer a consideration, Trump’s final two 
years are likely to focus on his legacy, entrenching what he views as his defining 
achievements. 
We are looking at a further strengthening of border enforcement with expanded 
deportations. Tariff policy is likely to be increasingly focused on incentivising foreign 
partners to align with US international policy, together with more defence spending 
demanded of NATO members. Trump will also feel emboldened to push hard 
economically and militarily against Iran with demands that other countries contribute to 
those efforts. Deregulation is likely to be a theme, while fast-tracking AI/technology 
innovation and incentivising manufacturing and energy production (both fossil and 
nuclear) will be the main policies aimed at boosting economic growth. 
Fiscal worries are likely to intensify, though. While few expect the president to follow 
through with his promise of $5,000 checks for returning Republicans to Congress, more 
defence spending will not be fully offset by efficiency savings or lower healthcare 
expenditure. Fiscal sustainability fears will intensify. At the same time, an escalation of 
military action in the Middle East could prompt significant energy market disruption, 
resulting in higher prices. Should the president force through “Trump dividend” 
payments, the Federal Reserve will come under even more market pressure to respond 
with higher interest rates, raising fears of a boom-bust cycle. 
The market response to this outcome would be upward pressure on bond yields right 
along the curve. The front end reflects an intensification in the rate hike risk. The back 
end is spooked by the prospect of a further widening in the fiscal deficit and reactive 
tightening by the Fed. The 10yr yield likely pops above 5.5% and stays above, while the 
2yr yield rises too and holds at around 5%. There is a contrarian possibility here that an 
emboldened Republican Party decides to act on the fiscal deficit, ultimately helping drive 
bond yields back down. But it’s more likely that such an action would require a bad pain 
trade first for Treasuries to trigger such action. 
Cyclically, this would be the most positive for the dollar on the back of the looser fiscal, 
tighter monetary policy outcome. Higher bond yields would continue to pressure-test 
the global financial system, with emerging market currencies and perhaps the euro too, 
via French government bonds, coming under further pressure. 
Scenario 2: Trump constrained 
Democrats win control of the House, Republicans retain the Senate 
The House, where officials serve two-year terms, is currently split between 219 
Republicans and 214 Democrats, with 2 seats vacant. Pollsters and prediction markets 
appear confident the Democrats will win here with relative ease. The Senate, currently 
split between 53 Republicans and 45 Democrats with 2 independents who vote with the 
Democrats, is a much closer call. The Democrats need to flip 4 of the 22 Republican 
seats and hold on to all 13 of their seats that are up for election to gain control of the 
Senate. 
Even if the Democrats fail to win the Senate, they will stifle President Trump’s legislative 
agenda over his final two years in power. Spending and tax proposals will be gridlocked 
while many Democrats will use the House victory to justify stalling data centre 
construction in their constituencies. There will be more scrutiny of technology 
companies and AI development, with hearings before Congress being demanded. Fossil 
fuel producers may face similar challenges.

### Page 14

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

14 
A key question is whether a constrained President Trump, entering the final phase of his 
tenure, becomes more willing to accept political or market disruption in pursuit of his 
objectives. Given the gridlock in Congress, the president will only be able to implement 
policy where he can use Executive Powers: 1) Foreign policy and diplomacy, including 
trade policy, 2) national security and defence, 3) law enforcement and regulatory policy, 
4) appointments of personnel, and 5) administration and management policy. This 
suggests Iran and tariffs/trade policy are likely to be the focus for the president, but the 
Democrats could use Congressional power to try and restrain his actions. 
Fiscal dynamics will remain a concern, although hitting the debt ceiling could act as a 
temporary brake. It will likely be an issue by mid-summer 2027 with little ground for 
compromise between the two sides in Congress. This risks an extended government 
shutdown, potentially exceeding last October’s shutdown, which depressed GDP growth 
in 4Q 2025 by a full percentage point. The Federal Reserve risks “flying blind” again with 
no government economic statistics being collated or released. 
It is the House where impeachment proceedings are started and a new round of action 
is possible. To be impeached, 67 of 100 Senators must vote in favour, which is likely not 
going to happen given there won’t be enough Democrats (and supportive Republicans) 
to force it through. Nonetheless, the Democrats could use it as a way to constrain the 
president, but it risks toxifying the political situation in the US. Rather than the president 
facing such action, it has been mooted that the Democrats could launch investigations 
into members of his cabinet. 
This outcome is broadly in line with the central market discount. The Democrats have 
one foot in the door, but remain broadly powerless to make meaningful change. Policy 
stasis leaves the bond market to balance the trade-off between a still elevated fiscal 
deficit and perhaps some taming of inflation concerns. We will probably see front-end 
yields easing lower while back-end yields maintain an upside bias, albeit within our 
preferred 5.25% to 5.5% range. 
The dollar broadly prices in this scenario and a split Congress probably will not be 
significant enough to drag attention away from elevated inflation and a Fed intent on 
delivering at least one more rate hike. A prolonged government shutdown could start to 
weigh on the dollar in the second half of 2027, however. 
Scenario 3: Democrat clean sweep 
Democrats win control of both the House and the Senate 
Given the polling and President Trump’s poor personal approval ratings, this is the most 
likely outcome priced in prediction markets, although political analysts remain on the 
fence between this and scenario 2. 
The implications are similar to scenario 2, but Democrats would likely feel even more 
confident about pushing back against President Trump’s policies on Iran, support of 
Israel and any escalation of tariffs. They will be applying pressure on the president to 
sound tougher towards Russia and to provide support to Ukraine. Domestically, 
Democrats in Congress will try and pass legislation that improves healthcare access in 
return for some acquiescence surrounding the president’s demands for higher defence 
spending – a government shutdown could reflect badly on the Democrats if they have 
control of both the House and the Senate. Fiscal concerns will persist. 
A very strong showing for the Democrats that leads to a sizeable majority would 
heighten the chances of a third impeachment of President Trump, although it would 
need a sweeping victory to offer any chance of a conviction. 
More fundamentally, a heavy defeat in the House and Senate could prompt figures 
within the Republican Party to take the view that it is time to open the debate on its

### Page 15

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

15 
future direction. With President Trump’s influence set to fade, leading lights within the 
party, most notably JD Vance and Marco Rubio, may attempt to shore up support for 
their own campaigns as they look to the 2028 presidential election. Similarly, a large 
Democrat victory will intensify speculation over whether the party’s candidate should be 
a traditionalist or whether the Left have the momentum to provide the 2028 candidate. 
This outcome is not dramatically different from the previous scenario. Here the 
Democrats have a mandate to secure some change, but will remain constrained by an 
obstructive executive branch. Front-end yields come under upside pressure. Back-end 
yields possibly see a silver lining from more tariffs acting as a tax hike, but at the same 
time pressured by inflation. The 10yr risks getting towards 5.5%, but without breaking 
above. 
The dollar could see a little weakness here as an emboldened Democratic Party takes 
greater issue with Republican policy, including deregulation and the AI build-out. 
What it means for Europe and China 
Should Republicans pull off a huge surprise and retain control of Congress, political 
pressure on Europe will increase. The risk of a forced deal between Russia and Ukraine, 
to the disadvantage of Ukraine and Europe, increases and could undermine economic 
sentiment in Europe. A new escalation in the Middle East would push up energy prices 
and harm the European economy. Europe’s latest shift towards more protectionist trade 
policies could bring new global trade tensions. 
Should there be a split Congress with the Democrats controlling the House, and Trump 
does indeed focus on foreign and trade policy, volatility and uncertainty in the global 
economy could increase. This will clearly affect Europe. At the same time, with only two 
years left in office, the TACO (Trump always chickens out) phenomenon could be an 
increasing part of Europe’s considerations. The risk of an unwarranted economic 
accident increases as Trump’s policies become more aggressive vis-à-vis Europe and 
Europe’s own stance hardens (hoping for TACO and an end to Trump’s term in office). 
If there is a Democrat clean sweep, the sense that Trump’s time is coming to an end 
could be all the greater and, under this scenario, some European countries could choose 
to slow the pace of defence spending increases given domestic fiscal considerations. 
Regardless of the election outcome, China’s main response will be wait-and-see, as it will 
react to actual policy changes rather than just potential shifts within Congress. The main 
potential trigger points would be Taiwan arms sales, tariffs and other trade/investment 
restrictions. 
Personal ties between the two leaders appear good and if the Republicans retain control 
of Congress, Trump’s attention will be spread across a broader range of issues. This 
would make rocking the boat with China appear a little less likely. However, if President 
Trump is constrained by a Democrat-controlled Congress, then his focus will be 
channelled towards foreign and trade policy, meaning China could end up in the 
crosshairs again. The trade truce is set to expire in January, and it seems like both sides 
are not interested in going back to the mutually destructive peak tariff scenario we had 
in 2025, but it cannot be ruled out. If the Democrats win both the House and the Senate, 
it would send a signal to China that it is time to start looking beyond 2028, triggering a 
revaluation of its long-term US strategy.

### Page 16

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

16

The Ministry of the Economy and Finance in Bercy, Paris. Even if the current budget proposal is adopted, fiscal 
uncertainty will not disappear in France

France is in the eye of the storm. Its public finances have deteriorated significantly, the 
budget faces a difficult parliamentary process, and the presidential election offers no 
clear prospect of political stabilisation. Economic conditions provide little support; we 
expect GDP growth of only 0.3% in 2026 and 0.6% in 2027. 
France’s debt dynamics are particularly unfavourable. Over the past 15 years, its public 
debt ratio has risen significantly, while many other European countries have reduced 
theirs. French public debt reached 119% of GDP in mid-2026 and is expected to increase 
to 121.7% in 2027. Persistent primary deficits, rising interest payments and weak growth 
mean that debt will continue to rise without sustained fiscal adjustment. 
The budget would limit the deterioration, not stabilise debt 
The draft budget aims to reduce the public deficit to 5% of GDP in 2027, after 5.4% in 
2026 and 5.1% in 2025. This requires €43bn of new measures. Without them, the 
government estimates that the deficit would reach around 6.5% next year. 
This is a substantial adjustment and is consistent with France’s European expenditure 
trajectory. But a 5% deficit remains too high to stabilise debt, particularly with weak 
growth and a rising interest bill. Even if implemented in full, the budget would only limit 
the deterioration; the debt ratio would continue to rise. 
The budget is likely to pass, but concessions may be needed 
The parliamentary process can run until 17 December under the 70-day constitutional 
timetable. Without a majority in the National Assembly, the government is unlikely to 
secure approval in a normal vote. Our base case is that it uses Article 49.3 to adopt the 
budget without one, potentially after making concessions to ensure that a subsequent 
no-confidence motion fails. This could reduce the final fiscal adjustment. 
We, nevertheless, expect the government to survive. Opposition parties have little 
incentive to trigger another political crisis shortly before the presidential election, take 
Charlotte de Montpellier 
Senior Economist, France and Switzerland 
charlotte.de.montpellier@ing.com

France’s 2027 budget is only the first 
fiscal test  
France’s 2027 budget would prevent a sharp deterioration in the public finances, but it 
would not be enough to stabilise public debt. Its adoption would provide some relief, 
although fiscal and political risks are likely to remain well beyond next year's presidential 
election

### Page 17

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

17 
responsibility for the resulting instability and risk a further deterioration in the public 
finances. 
Alternatively, the government could let the deadline expire and impose its original 
budget by ordinance. It would first have to survive any pre-emptive no-confidence 
motion. Once the ordinances were adopted, however, the budget would remain in force 
even if the government subsequently fell. 
Failing either outcome, a special law would ensure continuity of the state. But the 
deadlock could last until the summer and, without corrective measures, cause a sharp 
deterioration in the public finances. 
France therefore faces another two months of budget uncertainty. Adoption would be a 
relative success, preventing the deficit from rising towards 6.5%. But concessions could 
dilute the adjustment, while even the original package would not stabilise debt. 
Fiscal uncertainty will persist in 2027 
Even if the budget is adopted, fiscal uncertainty will not disappear. The next government 
will still have to implement further reforms. Yet the presidential election on 18 April and 
2 May 2027 will probably be followed by legislative elections, which could again produce 
a fragmented National Assembly and make those reforms difficult to pass. 
There is also little clarity on the future fiscal strategy. Few prospective candidates have 
presented sufficiently detailed plans to stabilise public finances, and almost none appear 
willing to tackle pensions, despite their being the largest component of public spending 
and central to France’s structural fiscal problem. 
The National Rally, which leads first-round polls, has made fiscal consolidation a priority. 
But its plan, in our view, relies on very optimistic macroeconomic assumptions and 
difficult-to-deliver savings, as discussed in our assessment of its fiscal programme. The 
far-left La France Insoumise and its candidate, Jean-Luc Mélenchon, support cancelling 
part of the debt held by the central bank and pursuing an expansionary fiscal policy. 
There is even less visibility on who will emerge from the centre, the right and the rest of 
the left, or on their fiscal plans. 
The 2027 budget will be a critical first step in containing France’s fiscal deterioration. 
Whether that adjustment can be sustained and extended after the election remains 
much more uncertain.

### Page 18

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

18

ECB President Christine Lagarde

The ECB’s most powerful tools are the ones it has never used. They are the tools that the 
ECB developed after Mario Draghi’s ‘whatever it takes’ speech. Tools that showed 
‘whatever it takes’ was something markets could believe in. The Outright Monetary 
Transactions programme during the euro crisis and the Transmission Protection 
Instrument (TPI) after the pandemic both shared the same objective: to enable 
potentially unlimited purchases of government bonds if needed. 
Political and fiscal woes in France and the widening of the French spread have now 
raised the question of whether the ECB could intervene through TPI, which allows it to 
purchase securities issued by member states whose financing conditions have 
deteriorated for reasons not justified by country-specific fundamentals. However, before 
activating it, the Governing Council considers a cumulative set of criteria to determine 
whether the countries concerned are pursuing sound and sustainable fiscal and 
macroeconomic policies. In practice, this includes compliance with the EU fiscal 
framework, which would, at a minimum, require the proposed French budget to be fully 
adopted. 
The ECB has nevertheless left itself some discretion, stating that the intervention criteria 
“will be dynamically adjusted to the unfolding risks and conditions to be addressed”. This 
brings back memories of the famous “because it’s France” by former European 
Commission President Jean-Claude Juncker. Generally speaking, the ECB will be in a 
difficult position: intervening too quickly in support of France may be viewed 
unfavourably by other member states and interpreted as monetary financing of the 
debt of a country unwilling to follow the rules. That argues strongly against early 
intervention. Also, don’t forget that the leadership changes at the ECB over the next 
months (Schnabel, Lane, Lagarde) could also affect how the ECB views TPI. 
Given the challenges of justifying the activation of TPI at present, we expect the ECB to 
adopt a three-pronged approach to address the French crisis. 
Carsten Brzeski 
Global Head of Macro and Chief 
Economist, Eurozone, Germany, Austria 
carsten.brzeski@ing.de

The ECB's options in dealing with 
France's fiscal woes 
The bar for activating the ECB's bond-buying programme remains high. However, a 
further deterioration in France's situation could eventually force the ECB to demonstrate 
whether its 'whatever it takes' commitment still holds

### Page 19

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

19 
The first line of defence is a more dovish approach to policy rates. As bond markets are 
currently taking over the ECB’s job of inserting a more restrictive monetary stance on 
the economy, there is less need for the ECB to hike rates aggressively, or to hike rates at 
all. In fact, the ECB has already started to prepare this option, as ECB president Christine 
Lagarde recently noted that “while growth has been resilient, since our last meeting 
long-term interest rates have risen notably, which will slow growth and reduce pass-
through by more than projected in our September exercise". 
The second line of defence would be a covert return to bond purchases. The ECB could 
instead pause quantitative tightening temporarily and reinvest maturing bonds in its 
portfolio “flexibly”, sending a positive signal to bond markets. This had already happened 
during the Covid period. 
Last but not least, and in our view, the ultimate option in the case of more contagion in 
eurozone bond markets, the ECB would eventually trigger TPI. As long as France remains 
non-compliant with the European fiscal rules, the ECB might even consider purchasing 
bonds from almost all eurozone countries, except for France. 
All in all, the current French situation is simply not enough for the ECB to trigger TPI. 
There are other ways to tackle any unwarranted impact from higher bond yields on the 
broader eurozone economy. However, a further escalation could eventually force the 
ECB to prove whether its 'whatever it takes' commitment still holds.

### Page 20

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

20

While some parts of the US economy continue to thrive, others are feeling the strain

Growing divergences in US sector performance 
The US economy continues to perform strongly, although it masks growing divergences 
between sections of the economy. In terms of the consumer, high-income households 
continue to spend strongly, boosted by large wealth gains in recent years, while middle 
and lower incomes are being squeezed by slowing income growth and elevated 
inflation. This is underscored by the breakdown in the relationship between sentiment 
and spending, with household stress highlighted by declines in the saving ratio and 
rising loan delinquencies. The Federal Reserve’s Survey of Consumer Finances reported 
that 8.6% of US households now spend more than 40% of their income on debt 
obligations. 
University of Michigan consumer confidence & consumer spending growth YoY%

Source: Macrobond, ING

The job market sees similar bifurcation. Government, leisure and hospitality and private 
education and healthcare services are adding jobs while other sectors are seeing flat to 
James Knightley  
Chief International Economist, Americas 
james.knightley@ing.com

Strong US growth masks growing splits 
High-income household spending and robust tech investment remain the key drivers of 
US growth. Higher borrowing costs are intensifying the stresses elsewhere, and this is 
prompting the Federal Reserve to take a gradual approach to policy tightening

### Page 21

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

21 
modest declines in employment levels. One sector showing signs of revival is 
manufacturing, with job gains of 72,000 year-to-date after three consecutive years of 
declines. Again, performance varies, with advanced manufacturing output 
(electronics/technology, transport and aerospace, speciality chemicals, pharma and 
power) experiencing robust growth, while more commoditised manufacturing, where it 
is difficult to charge a premium for Made-in-America products, is seeing output fall. 
Then there is tech capex, which continues to soar amid the insatiable appetite to invest 
in data centres to facilitate growing AI demand. Power investment is also performing 
strongly, but outside of tech, we see little growth. 
Slow and steady from the Fed 
The growing split in performance is being intensified by higher borrowing costs, 
particularly higher Treasury yields, which are putting more pressure on interest rate-
sensitive sectors. The housing market is facing strife, with mortgage rates now above 
7.5% and housing transactions on par with the downturn experienced during the Global 
Financial Crisis. Auto sales are also cooling. 
This has raised questions about how effective monetary policy is right now in containing 
inflation. Federal Reserve interest rate increases won’t reopen the Strait of Hormuz, and 
they are probably not going to do much to restrain the big tech companies from 
spending aggressively on AI-related activities. However, it is increasingly hurting the 
weaker parts of the economy. 
Inflation fears to rapidly fade once a Middle East deal is done 
Fed officials have alluded to this risk, with some senior members suggesting they favour 
slow and steady tightening. We currently expect one more rate rise, which we expect to 
come at the December FOMC meeting. We don’t anticipate any shift in the US-Iran 
conflict ahead of the midterms (see our extended piece on what the election could 
mean), but with polls suggesting Democrats will at least gain control of the House, this 
will restrict the president’s ability to pass legislation, leaving him to focus on foreign 
policy after the election. Assuming we do see an agreement to improve Persian Gulf 
energy flows, this should significantly ease global inflation pressures, and we don’t think 
the Fed will go further. However, the risk is clearly tilted towards a longer-lasting conflict 
rather than one that is resolved imminently. This means we would not rule out an 
additional rate hike in the first quarter of 2027. 
Outside of energy and tech, inflation pressures are cooling. Housing is the largest 
component of the inflation basket by weight and the downturn here is increasingly likely 
to depress the shelter metrics. Furthermore, we are in a less onerous tariff regime than 
in 2025, while wage growth continues to cool given the low-hire, low-fire economy. 
So, when flows through the Strait of Hormuz normalise, we expect market concerns 
about inflation to recede. Any additional Fed rate hikes over and above what we expect 
are likely to be swiftly reversed once an agreement is reached.

### Page 22

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

22

Higher energy and borrowing costs continue to weigh on the eurozone economic outlook

October is the cruelest month 
October is budget season in Europe, when cash-strapped member states must show 
they can put their finances in order. France’s minority government has proposed a 
budget that curbs expenditure growth but would still fail to stabilise the debt ratio next 
year. With presidential elections due in April and May 2027 and street protests 
mounting, few parties are willing to back austerity. We still expect the government to 
invoke Article 49.3 to pass the budget without parliamentary approval, but tensions may 
rise first. 
As for the rest of the eurozone, contagion has already widened bond spreads in other 
highly indebted member states, tightening eurozone financial conditions. This will 
increase pressure on governments to engage in austerity measures, resulting in a less 
accommodative fiscal stance in 2027. 
Growth likely to slow temporarily 
Energy prices remain relatively high, while wage growth is failing to keep pace with 
inflation. Households drew on savings in the second quarter and may need to do so 
again to support spending. Yet signs of a weakening labour market and renewed falls in 
consumer confidence point to softer consumption growth in the coming quarters. 
Residential construction may also gradually feel the impact of higher bond yields. 
Business sentiment suggests GDP growth remained moderate in the third quarter, but 
strengthening headwinds are likely to weaken growth in the fourth quarter and the first 
quarter of next year. Following a very strong second quarter, artificially boosted by Irish 
GDP figures, we now expect average growth of 1.0% in 2026. Assuming energy prices 
normalise in 2027, growth should average 1.2% next year. 
Peter Vanden Houte 
Chief Economist, Belgium, Luxembourg, 
Eurozone

Second-round effects remain more moderate than in 2022-23 
Survey on selling price expectations

Source: LSEG Datastream

Second-round inflation effects remain moderate 
The eurozone's harmonised index of consumer prices (HICP) inflation rose to 3.8% in 
September, with underlying inflation at 2.5%. Energy prices are likely to lift the headline 
rate further in the coming months, while food prices, which are highly sensitive to 
energy costs, are also trending higher. Inflation is therefore likely to end the year above 
4%. 
At the same time, second-round effects remain muted. They may still emerge, but we 
do not expect a repeat of the full-blown inflation wave of 2022-23. Business surveys 
show that selling-price expectations have risen less than they did four years ago, 
suggesting firms have less pricing power as consumers turn more cautious. We expect 
inflation to fall back to an average of 2.5% next year. 
One last rate hike? 
We still expect one more European Central Bank rate hike, although higher bond yields 
are already doing much of the ECB’s work. Markets have moved towards our view, 
scaling back expectations from almost four increases to around one. Given the bond 
market unrest, we also expect the ECB to halt quantitative tightening before year-end, 
echoing earlier moves by the Federal Reserve and the Bank of England.

Trade ties may be calmer, but Beijing's global challenges are far from over

State Council meeting triggers new targeted policy support 
China's State Council meeting on 28 September called for stronger countercyclical policy 
adjustments. The following day, the People’s Bank of China cut the one-year pledged 
supplementary lending rate by 25bp to 1.5%. It also increased re-lending quotas for 
technology and equipment upgrades and for rural and small businesses. Beijing 
announced the first-ever nationwide mortgage interest subsidy for first-time 
homebuyers to boost the struggling property market. 
The package maintains an accommodative policy direction but relies on targeted tools 
and fiscal-monetary coordination rather than broad-based rate cuts. 
Overall, these moves are likely to offer modest support to investment and housing at a 
time when credit demand is likely to remain under pressure amid weak sentiment. 
There remains room for further support measures, such as policies to boost employment 
and incomes and other measures to boost domestic demand. Should economic 
momentum remain subdued into year-end, policymakers may respond with further 
fiscal stimulus and a broader easing of monetary policy. 
External trade ties stable for now, but risks remain 
President Xi’s visit to the US in September continued the “constructive strategic stability” 
framework agreed in May. It secured a short-term extension of the trade truce to 
January 2027, and $30bn of tariff cuts on both sides. 
It’s possible the leaders will meet again at the APEC summit in Shenzhen on 18-19 
November and at the G20 meetings in Florida on 14-15 December. 
Aside from the US, possible tensions with the EU have also grabbed the spotlight over 
the past month. A joint letter from German Chancellor Friedrich Merz and French 
President Emmanuel Macron calling for tougher trade tools signals increasing risk that 
we could see trade barriers come into effect. EU-China trade talks concluded with an

China’s recent State Council meeting appeared to signal a more proactive policy push

initial agreement to ease tensions. It includes reducing hybrid vehicle exports from China 
to the EU, improving market access, and increasing medical equipment imports from the 
EU to China. This should keep trade ties stable for now, but further negotiations are still 
likely ahead. 
With the EU representing around 14-15% of China’s total exports, the second-largest 
export market after ASEAN, maintaining stable trade ties remains important for China's 
outlook. 
China’s inflation remains the exception to the rule 
We’ve seen inflation rise in many economies across the world. But prices in China have 
largely remained under control. Most worry about a return to deflation more than out-
of-control inflation. 
Two factors are curbing China’s inflation: food and housing. Together, these represent 
over half of China’s CPI basket. Food inflation has been kept low amid an extended 
downturn in China’s pork price cycle. Supply has been aided by advancements in hog 
megafarms, low feed prices, and general efforts to maintain high levels of food self-
sufficiency. Housing prices, meanwhile, have declined amid China’s multi-year property 
bust. 
On net, China’s inflation looks likely to end the year solidly in positive territory. We 
expect 0.9% CPI inflation in 2026, firmly positive after three years of flirting with 
deflation. However, it will once again fall short of the target, raising questions about 
whether China has successfully escaped deflation. While we believe the worst is likely 
behind us, downside risks remain. 
PPI inflation, on the other hand, averaged 2.0% in the first eight months of the year, 
peaking at 4.1% year-on-year in June. This suggests industrial prices have moved out of 
deflation, driven by upstream and select technology sectors. Yet this level is generally 
still a fair bit lower than in other Asian economies this year.

26

The Bank of England has held rates steady throughout the Iran war, but we think that is about to change

Elevated natural gas prices point to a November rate hike 
Having kept rates on hold throughout the Iran war, we now expect the Bank of England 
to deliver two rate hikes, in November and February, for two key reasons: 
First, Bank officials have made it increasingly clear that a hike was likely if energy prices 
didn't significantly fall ahead of the November meeting. 
Since early on in the crisis, the Bank has made it clear that, absent the war, it would 
have cut rates at least twice this year. So by simply not doing that, it has, in effect, 
tightened policy by 50bp – akin to what the European Central Bank has done so far. A 
Bank of England hike in November could therefore be placed into the same category as 
the ECB’s well-telegraphed December move. 
Second, it’s also clear that the inflation profile is likely to become uncomfortably high for 
the Bank. Last summer, it argued that when inflation surpasses 4%, we are statistically 
more likely to get second-round effects. And though headline inflation is currently 3.1%, 
a circa 20% rise in household electricity/gas bills forecast for January could get us there. 
That’s based on an average of gas futures prices between mid-August and mid-
November, so with two-thirds of that window already behind us, there’s only limited 
scope for that rise in bills to be mitigated if wholesale prices suddenly fall. And crucially, 
our commodities team don’t think they will. 
Combined with a likely increase in food inflation, it means we’re now briefly forecasting 
4% CPI in early 2027. The Bank’s own November forecasts are likely to show that too. 
James Smith 
Economist, Developed Markets 
james.smith@ing.com

Why we now expect two Bank of 
England rate hikes 
Inflation looks set to briefly top 4% next year and that's likely to trigger a November 
insurance rate hike from the Bank of England. We tentatively expect that to be followed 
by another hike in February. But we're still sceptical that we'll see big second-round 
effects on inflation. We think rates could be cut again by late 2027

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

27 
There's no sign that the energy shock is broadening out

Source: Macrobond, ING calculations

That said, there are still good reasons to think inflation won’t become the problem the 
hawks fear. Last autumn provides a useful precedent: headline CPI got perilously close to 
4% yet triggered no discernible pressure elsewhere in the inflation basket. The 
fundamental reasons for that – like the fragile jobs market and tighter fiscal backdrop – 
haven't changed. And while it’s early days, there’s no sign yet that the Iran war is 
putting upward pressure on energy-sensitive parts of the inflation basket – quite the 
opposite. 
Then there's the forthcoming Autumn Budget, announced a week before November's 
meeting. This looks increasingly like a non-event – and not something that's likely to add 
to the case for higher rates. In fact, the Chancellor is likely to do what he can – which 
isn't much – to cap the rise in inflation next year. That might include taking a greater 
chunk of 'policy costs' out of household energy bills and funding them out of taxation, 
thus mechanically limiting the pass-through of gas prices into inflation. We suspect the 
Treasury will also resist the temptation to boost next year's minimum wage rise over 
and above independent recommendations (c.4%). 
What this all means is that, assuming the BoE does hike, it’s likely to be cast as an 
insurance move. And, like any insurance policy, it can be cancelled later on. Assuming 
we’re right that there won’t be big second-round effects, we think rate cuts could be on 
the agenda before the end of 2027. 
That's a very different story from the one markets are telling. Investors are pricing in 
rates rising by a full percentage point over the next year and remaining at those 
elevated levels for years afterwards.

Asia's economy remains supported by a robust AI investment cycle

AI tailwinds continue, but momentum is moderating 
Asia's growth has remained resilient this year, outperforming expectations, largely 
driven by a stronger-than-expected AI investment cycle. The AI-related upswing has 
provided a significant boost to trade, manufacturing activity, and exports, particularly in 
the region's semiconductor and electronics supply chains. Economies with greater 
exposure to the AI ecosystem, including Taiwan, Korea, Malaysia and Singapore, have 
been among the key beneficiaries, with strong external demand helping offset still-soft 
domestic demand. 
The AI boost is still supporting growth, but momentum is beginning to moderate, with 
AI-related export growth showing early signs of cooling. At the same time, the benefits 
of the AI cycle remain concentrated in a relatively narrow set of sectors, limiting the 
extent to which stronger external demand has translated into a broader domestic 
growth recovery across the region. 
Looking ahead to 2027, growth could soften if the AI tailwind fades and weak domestic 
consumption becomes a more visible drag on activity. Household spending across much 
of Asia remains constrained by still-high interest rates, soft labour income growth, and 
weak consumer confidence. As the boost from AI-led exports gradually normalises, 
underlying domestic demand conditions are likely to play a larger role in determining 
growth outcomes. 
Inflation dynamics point to further policy tightening across Asia 
Inflation is accelerating across most of Asia. Food and fuel inflation has begun to 
accelerate in more import-dependent economies, notably the Philippines. Across most 
of North Asia, fuel and food inflation remain relatively contained, except for Taiwan. 
Hence, the impact of higher food and fuel prices is uneven across Asia. What is most 
relevant for central bankers is the transmission of these inflation pressures into core 
inflation. Core inflation has strengthened, especially in economies such as Taiwan,

Singapore, and the Philippines, pointing to firmer underlying price pressures. While in the 
Philippines and Singapore, the large transmission of global oil prices to domestic prices is 
the key driver, in Taiwan, the pressures appear more AI-driven. 
Looking ahead, El Niño and elevated fuel prices remain key upside risks to inflation. 
While the impact has been limited so far, signs are emerging that food and fuel price 
pressures are becoming more broad-based, particularly in the Philippines and Taiwan. 
Overall, headline inflation remains below central bank targets in Japan and China, but 
continues to exceed targets in Korea, Taiwan, and most Southeast Asian economies. As 
a result, we expect rate hikes in all Asian economies under our coverage, including 
Australia, with China the exception. 
FX implications – AI exporters vs oil importers 
On currencies, we remain bearish on those where the negative impact of higher oil 
prices outweighs the positive effects, if any, from the AI boom. South Asian currencies 
remain vulnerable to higher oil import costs and weaker current account balances. 
Hence, we expect depreciation pressures to persist for the Indian rupee, Philippine peso, 
and Indonesian rupiah. 
By contrast, we are mildly bullish on the Korean won, Singapore dollar, and Taiwanese 
dollar, which continue to benefit from strong AI-related exports and rising trade 
surpluses. These economies have seen external balances strengthen as demand for 
semiconductors and other technology products remains robust.

Central Europe is navigating resilient growth, persistent inflation risks and increasingly divergent monetary policy 
paths

### Page 31

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

Czech Republic: Accelerating prices amid decelerating growth 
Headline inflation has just started its climb to its peak and is expected to hit around 4% 
in February, as renewed price growth in the regulated and foodstuffs segment combines 
with persistently elevated fuel prices and core inflation. Part of the surge may be 
attributed to the low comparison base from early 2026, but we believe that the second-
round effects from the Hormuz conflict are starting to materialise as the turmoil gains a 
more permanent shape. 
In a textbook response to a major global supply shock, the economy is facing a period of 
elevated inflation, including spillovers beyond energy into other price categories, 
alongside a deceleration in economic activity. We expect economic growth to slow to 
around 1.8% this year before only gradually picking up thereafter. 
Real wage growth will be dampened by the surge in inflation, while rising mandatory 
expenses for food and energy will put a strain on household budgets. Furthermore, 
firms' pressured profitability will likely prevent excessive nominal wage gains over the 
upcoming year, as input costs continue to rise amid intense competition. The weaker 
koruna contributes to galloping import prices, and the CNB is starting to monitor the 
extent to which the exchange rate loosens overall monetary conditions. 
Combine the outlook for forceful imported inflation with the still-robust domestic price 
pressures, and you end up with a recipe for a higher policy rate needed to prevent 
inflation from getting out of hand for longer. As FX comes into the spotlight, the steps of 
the Fed and the ECB may shape the CNB reaction to a greater extent than before. The 
more hesitant the ECB becomes due to the fragile French fiscal situation, the more it 
may work in favour of the koruna. 
Hungary: Credibility is the next catalyst 
The Hungarian economy continues to move away from its prolonged stagnation, 
although the recovery is still far from spectacular. GDP has now expanded for five 
consecutive quarters, and we expect growth of 1.9% this year, driven mainly by 
consumption. Investment remains the missing piece, while net exports are still a drag, 
although new industrial capacities are increasingly supporting exports. Inflation, 
meanwhile, continues to surprise on the downside. Despite higher fuel prices, a weaker 
forint and elevated wage costs, headline inflation reached just 1.6% in September, while 
core inflation fell to 1.9%. We now expect average inflation of only 1.8% this year and 
around 3% in 2027. The new 2.5% inflation target from 2028 could provide an additional 
anchor for price-setting decisions. 
This benign inflation backdrop leaves room for further monetary easing, but the external 
environment remains the key constraint. After pausing at 5.50% in September, we 
expect the National Bank of Hungary to deliver another 25bp interest rate cut in 
December, followed by a further 100bp of easing in 2027. Before that, however, fiscal 
policy could provide the next major catalyst for Hungarian assets. We expect a credible, 
front-loaded medium-term consolidation plan, accompanied by the first official steps 
towards ERM II. If policymakers deliver, the resulting decline in Hungary’s risk premium 
could push EUR/HUF towards 350-355 in the short term and trigger another leg of the 
bond rally. Thereafter, renewed monetary easing should put the forint on a mild 
depreciation path, but with credibility improving, external balances strengthening and 
the euro adoption narrative increasingly acting as a policy anchor, we see Hungary 
entering a new phase of convergence. 
Romania: Politics remains on the front page 
Stagflationary pressures and political uncertainties continue to dominate the Romanian 
macro story. Detailed GDP figures for the second quarter confirmed that private 
consumption was the main culprit, while investments provided one of the strongest

### Page 32

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

32 
positive contributions to growth on record, on the back of accelerating Recovery and 
Resilience Facility-related investments. Concerning price pressures, we expect inflation 
to end the year at 6.5% and average 8.5% throughout 2026, with potential upside risks. 
While an investment-driven third quarter is on the cards as well, a continuation of public 
investment activity through other EU programmes like SAFE and Cohesion funds means 
that new capital formation should moderate but not fall off a cliff after RRF ends. On the 
fiscal front, the deficit remains the key positive element supporting Romania’s credit 
story. As of August, the fiscal balance stood at 2.89% of GDP (vs 4.50% in August 2025). 
A year-end value of 6.0% or less is within reach. 
Meanwhile, governance continues to be in “interim” mode, with agreements for a 
government formation still tricky to be reached. Escaping the downgrade threat in early 
October remains a key short-term win, although many must-do reforms continue to be 
on borrowed time. A fully empowered government and a credible medium-term fiscal 
framework remain necessary to reduce the risk of a downgrade.

Higher oil prices are strengthening Azerbaijan's economic buffers

Base is bliss: selectively dovish and FX-constructive 
Since the outbreak of Middle East tensions, the CIS-4 has weathered higher volatility, 
rising global inflation expectations and tighter core rates, easing some of our initial 
concerns. A gradual deterioration in ING’s global base case, or stabilisation, should 
therefore be broadly neutral for the regional outlook. Higher energy and freight costs lift 
imported inflation, but CIS-4 currencies, so far resilient to tighter global rates, should 
keep policy responses country-specific. 
Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan still have room for cautious monetary easing as inflation 
falls from high bases and strong currencies dampen external cost pressures. The tenge 
has strengthened 11% since the Iran war began, supported by capital inflows, while the 
backlog of state FX sales provides a medium-term cushion. The soum has gained 2% 
and should benefit from a catch-up in gold exports, supporting FX in the medium term. 
Kazakhstan’s inflation has slowed 3ppt this year to 9.3% year-on-year, allowing the 
National Bank of Kazakhstan to cut the base rate from 18.00% to 16.25%. Uzbekistan’s 
inflation fell almost 1ppt to 6.4%, while its policy rate remains at 14.00%. Meanwhile, 
inflation remains above both 5% targets, which, combined with domestic demand-side 
pressures, limits further cuts to 1.0-1.5ppt up to the end of 2027. 
Azerbaijan is more stable: higher oil prices reinforce its buffers, while the manat peg 
limits imported inflation. With CPI within the 4±2% target range, we expect the policy 
rate to stay at 6.50%. 
Armenia remains the hawkish outlier. Tourism and remittance-supported demand has 
lifted inflation 1ppt this year to 4.3% YoY, above the 3% target. September’s 25bp hike to 
6.75% appears precautionary, but inflation approaching 5% could warrant another small 
move. 
Dmitry Dolgin 
Chief Economist, CIS 
dmitry.dolgin@ing.de

Our base case for the regional rates and currencies remains constructive and

rates could be put to the test through the exchange rate channel. While we don't expect 
a sell-off, high real rates will be required to keep attracting portfolio inflows

Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan retain relatively high real rates 
Real policy rates in CIS-4 (%), based on CPI over the past 12 months

Source: National sources, CEIC, Refinitiv, ING

Persistent global inflation and higher core rates would put CIS 
resilience to the test 
As the global context this year has a poor track record in fulfilling the base case or 
optimistic expectations, an alternative and more challenging scenario should be 
considered. A further spike in commodity prices, which would bring stronger inflation 
and materially higher core rates, would likely put CIS resilience to a test, with CIS macro 
sensitivities back in focus. 
Higher energy prices. Each additional $10/bbl could lift annual exports by 2-4% of GDP 
in Azerbaijan and Kazakhstan, although the FX effect is constrained by Azerbaijan’s peg 
and Kazakhstan’s export volumes and dividend-outflow dynamics. For Armenia and 
Uzbekistan, it would add around 0.5% of GDP to import costs. 
Imported inflation. Global food prices are already 6% higher YoY. We estimate that each 
10% increase could add around 1ppt to the inflation trend in Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan 
and 1.5-2.0ppt in Armenia and Azerbaijan. Broader exposure to imports from developed 
markets and the Middle East ranges from 26% in Armenia and 35% in Kazakhstan and 
Uzbekistan to around 45% in Azerbaijan. 
Higher core yields would weaken carry-driven support for the tenge and soum, reducing 
their insulation from imported inflation. But domestic buffers argue against an 
indiscriminate sell-off: Kazakhstan has high real rates and a backlog of state FX sales; 
Uzbekistan will be catching up on delayed gold exports; Armenia can fall back on its 
remittance inflows and comfortable reserves; and Azerbaijan can rely on its exceptional 
sovereign assets. 
In an adverse global scenario, we therefore see 1-2 ppt upside risk to our medium-term 
inflation outlook. The cost-driven nature of the shock may not require an immediate 
policy response, but central banks may need to protect real rates: Armenia could tighten 
further, Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan have less room to cut, and Azerbaijan’s reluctance 
to hike could be challenged.

EUR/USD looks set to stay pressured on wide rate differentials

Dollar enjoys some cyclical strength 
We see EUR/USD remaining in a 1.10-1.15 range over the next three to six months, as 
persistent US rate support and concerns over French debt keep real rate differentials 
tilted against the euro. 
It is hard to believe that EUR/USD traded above 1.20 in January. Those fears of the dollar 
losing its safe-haven status as European investors reacted to egregious US foreign policy 
have dissipated this year as US cyclical strength has become more evident. There are no 
signs of international investors leaving US markets, and by our calculations, foreigners 
have kept their share of US bond and equity markets steady at around 20%. 
As we argued at the time, 'sell America’ moments have occurred in the past and tend to 
be short-lived. Correlations now suggest the dollar is playing its safe haven role again. 
That dollar vulnerability at the start of the year was backed by a weak US employment 
story and the prospect of further easing. Today, the Federal Reserve is faced with a solid 
labour market, above-target inflation and the prospect of taking policy into mildly 
restrictive territory. While we are not looking for the central bank to deliver on all three 
hikes priced in over the next 12 months, we doubt that the current hawkish Fed pricing 
will leave the market until next spring. At that point, base effects look likely to bring 
inflation much closer to the Fed's 2% target. 
In contrast, the near 75bp of European Central Bank hikes priced into the market looks 
more precarious. Heavy losses in French debt dragged euro short-dated swap rates 
lower in early October, and investors are rightfully questioning how the European 
Central Bank plans to take policy into restrictive territory at the time of a looming debt 
crisis. 
Chris Turner 
Global Head of Markets and Regional Head 
of Research, UK & CEE 
chris.turner@ing.com

The dollar story improves 
We see limited scope for a near-term EUR/USD rebound. Key factors for EUR/USD now 
are a hawkish Fed and a re-pricing of the ECB cycle on French fiscal concerns, which have 
sent rate differentials to their widest levels since 2022. Neither of those factors appears 
set for an imminent reversal, leaving EUR/USD to languish in a new 1.10-1.15 range

That growing concern over French debt, which will probably be with us right through to 
presidential elections next April, should keep real rate differentials wide and keep 
EUR/USD under pressure. Higher oil and gas prices aren't helping the euro either. 
In all, we now see EUR/USD staying offered in a 1.10-1.15 range over the next three to 
six months, with downside risks should a sharp deterioration in the French debt story 
demand a greater risk premium for the euro.

Source: Refinitiv, ING

What could hurt the dollar? 
A key risk to our constructive dollar view is a renewed rise in FX hedge ratios among 
foreign investors. While confidence in the dollar has pushed hedge ratios back to very 
low levels, any renewed White House intervention in financial, trade or foreign policy 
could prompt investors to increase hedging and weigh on the dollar. 
A big topic for FX strategists over the last 18 months has been foreign investor FX hedge 
ratios on US assets. Data suggests these have returned to very low levels again as 
confidence has returned to the dollar story. As we witnessed briefly in August, when the 
US Treasury extended the duration of its Treasury buy-back operations, the dollar was 
hit by White House intervention in financial markets. Any fresh White House intervention 
in financial, trade or foreign policy, perhaps after the November midterms, could see the 
dollar come under pressure as investors raise hedge ratios again. 
On the subject of the midterms, the base case of the Democrats winning the House 
looks unlikely to move FX markets much. But a Democratic clean sweep of Congress, and 
what it means for a potential government shutdown, regulation, and constraints on the 
AI build-out, could prove a cyclical negative for the dollar.

### Page 37

Monthly Economic Update October 2026

37

The mood for US Treasuries has calmed. But don't get too comfortable 
Long-end yields have calmed in the past week or so. But pressures remain. The medium-
term issuance narrative stemming from fiscal largesse remains. That’s the 'bad'. Then 
there’s the 'ugly', in the guise of energy price pressure stemming from the Iran war. That 
has coincided with higher printed inflation. Arguably, this is the biggest direct impact, as 
it has prompted central bank rate hikes. And there is the 'good'. Higher real rates have 
been an important driver, and a part of this likely incorporates AI-driven productivity 
growth. If that last part was the central driver, we’d have little to worry about. But it’s 
the other, more sinister factors that have predominantly sparked long-end market 
jitters. 
The balance of probabilities suggests there is at least one more leg of long-end 
weakness still to play out. This could take the US 10-year yield back into the 5.25% to 
5.5% range. Other markets would likely move in tandem, mapping out similar, albeit less 
dramatic, blow-outs. With 5.25% looking like a mean-reversion level, we think the US 10-
year yield could overshoot towards 5.5% and remain elevated for a number of months 
as bond market jitters persist. This could quite easily extend into the first quarter of 
2027. 
For later in 2027, we think inflation will come down, and long rates should too. On the 
growth side, we have a 2% real growth forecast tendency for 2027. If we’re wrong and 
we’re on the verge of a macro boom (and/or persistently elevated inflation), that’s a 
whole other story, and it would be a route to 5.5-6%. The other way to get there could 
be the US fiscal story finally unravelling in a bad way, but we aren't quite sure we can 
call for that yet as a baseline view. 
For 2027, we have growth of 2% and inflation of 2% (or slightly above on both counts). 
That points to 4.75%, representing a mild upside bias relative to the 4.5% level we view 
as broadly neutral. But first, we need to get through 2026.

The eurozone remains in the throes of an uneasy holding pattern at 
dodgy levels 
The focus in the eurozone is increasingly insular, with France front and centre and 
setting the mood. Here too, there has been some market stability – but at 
uncomfortable levels. The standout move has been a doubling of the French 10yr swap 
spread from the 60bp area to the 120bp area. And French spreads to Germany are 
practically as wide as they've ever been since the dawn of the euro, even versus the 
immediate pre-EMU years. The safe narrative to reach for is the debt dynamics one, and 
the lack of ambition to correct it. Made worse, in fact, by the political options being 
offered for 2028. 
This is probably overdone, as a swing to the right (should it occur) could have some tail 
positives that aren't currently properly discounted. Look at the improvement seen in 
Italian spreads following the rise of Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni. It's been gradual but 
persistent, and has not been overly impacted by French concerns. In that sense, the 
Italian response has been low beta in characterisation, which is good. 
In fact, the first real bout of widening in French spreads can be traced back to President 
Emmanuel Macron's election gamble of mid-2024. Things subsequently calmed, but 
were not forgotten. The recent spike is an amplification and extension of an underlying 
tension that has not gone away. So, overdone? Probably, yes. But we also remain in a 
danger zone for bonds generally. Just like in the US, more overshoots to the upside are 
entirely foreseeable.

SUMMARY: AI euphoria meets macro headwinds
Tech remained a major driver of equity market performance in Q3. The
Mag 7 outperformed the broader market while the S&P 500 also

posted gains, lifted by mega-cap and tech stocks.
Still, tech’s path in Q3 was volatile. July began with a reversal of the AI
trade as investors questioned elevated valuations, the sustainability of
AI spending and the outlook for semiconductor demand. That led to a
large sell-off in chip stocks, with large falls in Q2 outperformers SK
Hynix and Samsung contributing to a sharp decline in Korea’s KOSPI.
The Philly SOX experienced its worst monthly performance since

2008.
By August, however, sentiment had improved. The S&P 500 reached
fresh record highs, even as US Treasury yields rose, supported by
stronger cloud earnings and signs of AI monetization. September
proved more challenging as higher oil prices, inflation concerns and
rising bond yields weighed on risk assets, particularly semiconductors.
The Fed also raised its policy rate by 25bp on Sep 16.
Yet, despite these macro headwinds, company guidance continued to

indicate strong AI infrastructure demand, with Micron’s stronger-than-
expected revenue guidance on Sep 30 providing further evidence of
robust demand for memory chips. Memory supply remains tight,
semiconductor capex is accelerating, and the industry’s attention has
increasingly turned to bottlenecks in optical networking and photonics,
as well as concerns around AI regulation and safety. Looking ahead,
tech is likely to remain an important driver of equity returns into Q4,

although elevated valuations and tighter financial conditions leave the
sector sensitive to earnings disappointments. Sources: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Updated Sep 30. Returns in local currency.
Q3 started with doubts about the AI trade but ended with renewed confidence

1. GLOBAL INDUSTRY BREAKDOWN
Tech hardware, including Lenovo, Advantech, and HP Inc, rebounded in Q3. This was partly driven by accelerating AI

server demand and the ongoing data center build-out, although continuing memory shortages raised costs for
hardware manufacturers. Cloud and server providers, like Oracle, Dell and HPE, also highlighted strong AI-related
demand. Semis and equipment were the weakest performers. Elsewhere, entertainment and telecoms posted modest
declines. Finally, in Q3 investor attention increasingly shifted to AI agents, with Meta’s Muse, Microsoft’s Copilot and

Salesforce’s Agentforce among the prominent offerings.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Median returns taken. Updated Sep 30.
Total returns for tech sectors over Q3 vs. YTD returns

### Page 5

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  Q3 2026 Tech Performance Review |  October 1, 20264
2. SEMICONDUCTORS
Chip stocks were hurt initially by a reversal of the AI trade and then by rising bond yields. The Philly SOX index fell
sharply in Q3, after its worst monthly performance in July (-21%) since 2008. Concerns about higher oil prices and
rising rates continued to weigh on the sector in Aug and Sep. SK Hynix was among the weakest performers, amid an

earnings disappointment relative to expectations and an unwinding of leveraged positions. Its Nasdaq ADRs began
trading in July. Despite these headwinds, the semis outlook remains supported by AI infrastructure demand. Within
semis, potential long-term beneficiaries include suppliers of AI infrastructure, memory including high-bandwidth
memory (HBM), advanced packaging, photonics and semiconductor equipment.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Updated Sep 30. Returns in local currency.

3.1 MAGNIFICENT 7: Microsoft’s earnings rebound
On July 30, Microsoft rose more than 15%, its strongest daily gain since 2008, following results released the previous

evening. The move added ~$450bn to its market cap, the largest one-day increase on record at the time. For context,
that daily increase is larger than the value of any company in Europe (except ASML).
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP. Updated Sep 30.

3.2 MAGNIFICENT 7: Market concentration
According to our equity strategists, from end-July to mid-September, tech-exposed stocks within the S&P 500 rose
14% while the remainder declined 3%. This divergence highlights the concentration of market returns in the tech -

exposed sector. In late July, our strategists anticipated a rotation back into technology, which remains underway.

Notably, most of US tech is near recovery from their 1yr highs, unlike

4. REGIONAL BREAKDOWN
After outperforming in Q2, mainland Chinese tech stocks posted large declines this quarter, hurt by July’s sell-off.
The CSI 300 tech series led the declines. Weakness also extended across Asia more broadly, with the KOSPI, Nikkei 225
and Hang Seng Tech down in Q3, driven by a chip sell-off and concerns over further Fed hikes. European tech equities
were more resilient, with the STOXX Europe 600 Tech Index down slightly lower in Q3 but still well above its year-end

level. Within European tech, Infineon underperformed, while Orange illustrated weakness in telecoms outside the index.

5. CRYPTO, AI & FINTECH
Our thematic indicators also faced macro headwinds in Q3, although the fintech and blockchain ETF shown
recovered during the quarter. Bitcoin also recovered strongly during Q3, trading around $83k in late Sep, despite the

setback when the Senate failed to advance the CLARITY Act on Sep 15.

6. M&A
Q3 saw lower announced deal value than Q2, against a backdrop of uncertainty around the upcoming US midterm
elections, ongoing geopolitical conflict and higher oil prices. Separately, several companies, including Oura and
Holtec, have delayed their IPOs, citing uncertainty. Still, there were some notable deals, including Nvidia’s agreement
to acquire Hugging Face for ~$12.93bn, announced on Sep 3, and AMD’s announced acquisition of World Labs for

$8.2bn on Sep 28.

營運成果回顧暨未來展望
5
1H26營收再創同期新高，AI產品組合升級帶動獲利提升：受惠於高階AI應用需求持續成長，1H26營
收達新台幣892億元，年增13.9%，再創歷年同期新高。其中，伺服器／光模塊／ IC載板合計營收年
增113%，營收占比達21.6%。隨高附加價值產品比重增加，1H26毛利率及營業利益率分別提升至
22.4%及7.0%，顯示公司成長動能正由營收規模擴張，進一步轉化為獲利提升。
1
2 AI算力建設開啟高階PCB長期成長週期，臻鼎憑藉完整技術布局邁入新一輪成長：隨下一世代AI平台
陸續量產，公司伺服器／光模塊業務自第二季起逐季增強，2026年相關營收目標維持成倍成長。中
長期而言，AI需求將由伺服器與光模塊進一步擴展至Edge AI及人形機器人等終端應用，持續提升高
階PCB的使用量、技術門檻與產品價值，公司對2026-2030 年的成長深具信心。
3 以業界最大的擴產規模打造下一階段營運動能：高階PCB產能建置與客戶認證均需較長前置期，唯
有糧草先行，才能掌握AI帶來的長期成長機會。公司2026年資本支出為新台幣800億+，2027-2028年

亦將維持較高投資水準，聚焦iHDI ／ mSAP、HLC、IC載板及高階軟板等高成長、高附加價值領域。
公司目前共有22棟新廠建設中、9棟新廠規劃中，新增產能將依客戶導入及量產進度，於2026至
2030年間分階段開出，逐步將資本投入轉化為營收與獲利貢獻。
4 禮鼎於2026年7月2日正式向港交所遞交主板上市申請：上市申請目前仍處於審查程序，相關資訊皆
以港交所正式刊載的文件為準。

### Page 22

全球生產基地加速擴產，效益將於2026年起逐步顯現
◼ 泰國一廠：
◼ 主要生產高階iHDI、HLC及光模塊產品，於3Q25進入量產。
◼ 量產爬坡順利，已通過多家客戶認證及量產，尚有多家伺服器與光模塊全球領先客戶正積極進行認證中。
◼ 7月已達損益兩平。
◼ 目前另有三棟新廠房及一棟機械鑽孔中心同步建置中，規劃將於4Q26起逐步進入試產階段。
◼ 淮安園區：
◼ 規劃至2026年底，淮安HA、HB、HC園區將有6棟高階PCB新廠完成建設。
◼ 另HD園區已於今年四月動土，規劃新建HDI/MSAP與HLC廠，8月10日封頂，預計明年三月加入生產。
◼ 深圳園區：
◼ 第三園區智慧製造基地於今年七月動土，預計興建三棟智慧製造廠房，重點布局AI伺服器用及光模塊用的
高階類載板，及AI終端用高階軟板等高端產品。
◼ 禮鼎深圳第二園區於今年九月正式動土，總投資金額不低於人民幣120億元，規劃建設數座工廠，聚焦
FCBGA等高階IC載板。新產能主要面向AI、高效能運算及先進封裝需求，進一步強化公司高階FCBGA產品的

研發、製造與規模化交付能力。

1
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
WESTPAC NOWCAST Q3
2026: FIRST ESTIMATE
Growth picks up despite headwinds
25 September 2026
Key points
• Westpac-Now points to GDP growth
of around 0.6%qtr in Q3 2026 (with a
70% confidence interval of 0.29%qtr to
0.91%qtr) and 2.3%yr. This is a step up

from the 0.4%qtr and 2.1%yr recorded
in Q2 2026 (see here) and implies that
economic momentum has strengthened.
•  Our Monthly Activity Index has
stabilised after declining sharply since
November 2025 and is now showing
signs of improvement in July. This
provides further evidence the economy
is proving more resilient than initially
feared. Rather than deteriorating further,
activity appears to be weathering the
impacts of the Middle East conflict and

higher interest rates relatively well.
•  The RBA is unlikely to be comfortable with
this combination of improving conditions
and firmer economic momentum. Growth
at 2.3%yr would be close to our estimate
of ‘trend’, but it is above the RBA’s estimate
at a time when the Bank is trying to
engineer a period of below-trend growth
to ensure inflation returns to target.
• Overall, the results are consistent with
our view that the economy is being
supported by powerful business investment

tailwinds, centred on data centres
and renewable energy (see here). This
investment surge is largely unrelated to
the domestic business cycle or interest
rates and is increasingly offsetting
weakness elsewhere in the economy.

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
In November 2025, we introduced Westpac-Now (Westpac
Nowcast (Westpac-Now): The Upswing Accelerates |
Westpac IQ), a real time measure of current activity in the
Australian economy. Westpac-Now draws on more than
60 high frequency economic and financial variables, using

advanced statistical techniques to provide a timely pulse
check on economic momentum. It is the first Australian
model to incorporate internal banking data, enhancing its
predictive power.
This note provides the first estimate of GDP growth for the
September quarter 2026, using domestic and international
data released up to and including the August Labour Force
Survey (released 24 September 2026). Future updates will be
published monthly (see Appendix A for the release schedule).

Westpac Monthly Activity Index: Activity improves
as momentum builds
After weakening sharply through late 2025 and early 2026,
the Westpac Monthly Activity Index is beginning to recover.
While activity levels remain subdued, recent readings suggest
the economy has moved beyond the period of broad-based
deterioration seen earlier in the year. On a rolling three-month
basis, the Index has continued to improve, consistent with
improving momentum.
The weakness through late 2025 and early 2026 reflected

a broad-based deterioration across confidence, spending,
business activity and labour market indicators as households
and businesses adjusted to a renewed inflation challenge
followed by interest rates moving higher once again. More
recent data suggest these conditions have begun to stabilise
Economic momentum building
Pat Bustamante
Senior Economist
and, in some cases, improve. Business and consumer
confidence, household spending, and labour market indicators

have generally proven more resilient than expected given the
headwinds facing the economy.
This improvement is evident in our heatmap, where the
intensity and breadth of negative readings have moderated
noticeably. While pockets of softness remain, particularly
in interest-sensitive areas of the economy, fewer indicators
are showing deteriorating momentum and a growing
number are now contributing positively to activity. Business-
related indicators remain among the strongest contributors,

consistent with the substantial support currently being
provided by investment in data centres, renewable energy and
related infrastructure.
Our indicator heatmap decomposes changes in the Monthly
Activity Index into group-level contributions, allowing us to
assess which components are driving movements in activity.
For ease of interpretation, indicators are grouped into broad
categories (see the Appendix for further details). For example,
the labour market category includes both official ABS Labour

Force Survey outcomes and timelier survey-based measures
of labour demand and job advertisements.
Westpac-Now: Resilient growth amid rising headwinds
Recall that Westpac-Now is a refined indicator that combines
our Monthly Activity Index with advanced econometric
techniques to estimate GDP growth. As previously noted,
the fit is not expected to be exact. Measured GDP includes
components that are not well captured by high-frequency
activity indicators, such as imputed rents for owner-occupiers,

housing related ownership transfer costs, public and business
inventories, and depreciation.

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
To bridge monthly and quarterly data, we use a MIDAS
framework, allowing us to combine mixed-frequency inputs
and incorporate partial information for the current quarter
when producing GDP forecasts.
Based on current model estimates, Westpac-Now’s final

estimate for Q2 2026 points to GDP growth of 0.6%qtr
(with a 70% confidence interval of 0.29%qtr to 0.91%qtr)
and 2.3%yr, representing a step up from the 0.4%qtr and
2.1%yr outcomes recorded in Q2 2026.  This suggests that
economic momentum has strengthened, with the economy
continuing to show resilience.
The key question going forward is whether higher interest
rates, elevated energy prices and heightened geopolitical
uncertainty in the Middle East will begin to weigh more

heavily on demand, offsetting the substantial support
currently being provided by business investment. Our
forecasts suggest that growth will moderate, particularly
following the RBA Board’s September rate hike, with activity
slowing rather than stalling.
Looking ahead, model dynamics suggest growth will pick up
towards trend, at around 0.6%qtr in Q4 2026 and Q1 2027.
However, these projections remain much more tentative,
given their reliance on projected inputs rather than realised

data. In addition, heightened uncertainty, particularly around
the lingering impacts of the Middle East conflict, means risks
around these forward estimates are larger than usual.
September Quarter data: Momentum improving
Data released in the first month of the September quarter
indicates that the economy has picked up momentum relative
to the previous quarter, with our nowcast estimating Q3
growth at 0.6%qtr, in line with the 0.6%qtr “pure forecast”

based solely on data available up to June 2026.
This is similar to what occurred in Q2 2026. Following the
large downside surprise in April 2026, which coincided with
sharp falls in consumer and business confidence, growth
estimates stabilised over the remainder of the quarter. The
weakness in Q2 was concentrated in April, with subsequent
data pointing to stabilisation rather than further deterioration.
Early Q3 data point to a similar pattern, with activity showing

signs of resilience despite headwinds.
Note that monthly activity estimates are more uncertain than
normal when inflation is shifting. Faster price growth can cause
nominal indicators, such as consumer spending, to rise more
strongly even though ‘real’, inflation-adjusted spending may
be softening (and vice versa). The inclusion of ‘real’ indicators,
such as employment and banking transaction volumes, helps
mitigate some – but not all – of this uncertainty.

Bottom line:
Westpac-Now points to GDP growth of around 0.6%qtr in
Q3 2026 (2.3%yr), indicating that the economy remains more
resilient than previously feared. Strong business investment,
particularly in data centres and renewable energy, continues to
provide an important offset to soft demand in interest-sensitive
sectors of the economy, including the housing market.
Looking ahead, higher interest rates, elevated energy prices
and ongoing geopolitical uncertainty are expected to weigh

on activity, particularly following the RBA’s September rate
hike. However, our central case remains one of slower growth
rather than a material downturn.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Appendix A: Westpac-Now Release Schedule (2026)
Going forward, Westpac Now will be published on the Friday following each ABS Labour Force Survey (LFS) release, at 11:30am AEDT/AEST (Canberra time).
Each release incorporates the latest labour market data alongside other high frequency indicators and provides an updated nowcast for quarterly GDP growth.

As the quarter progresses, each update should be interpreted as a progressively more complete estimate of that quarter’s economic performance.
Release date (Friday) LFS reference month Quarter focus Interpretation
16 October 2026 September 2026 Q3 2026 – second estimate Refined Q3 signal as activity data accumulate
20 November 2026 October 2026 Q3 2026 – final estimate Near complete read on Q3 activity
18 December 2026 November 2026 Q4 2026 – first estimate Initial assessment of Q4 momentum

Charts of the week: Diverging volatility

Strong earnings should resolve the bond and equity market divergence; French political risks remain largely contained  
S&P500 volatility index (VIX); US bond volatility index (MOVE)*   10-year government bond yield spreads over German bonds
